On the morning of 30 August, the governor’s office issued a press release stating that one police officer had been stoned to death and six Wazalendo followers had been killed, “some wounded, and 158 more arrested.”\n[123]\nOn 31 August, an internal army report cited by Agence France Presse gave a much higher toll: 48 protesters “neutralized”(meaning “killed” in security jargon), a police officer “lynched”, 75 protesters injured, and “around 168 people arrested.”\n[124]\nA few hours after this report was leaked to the press, the Congolese government issued a press release stating that 43 people had died, 56 persons were injured, and 158 were arrested. According to military court records, as of 18 September, when the official burial took place, 56 people had been counted dead in connection with the 30 August events, not including the police officer,\n[125]\nand their bodies had been held in three hospitals as follows:\n43 bodies at the Goma military hospital, including 29 formally identified and 14 unidentified;\nFour bodies at Heal Africa Hospital;\nNine bodies at CBCA Ndosho Hospital.\nAt the official burial site, however, Amnesty International only observed 54 graves.\n[126]\nAsked about this discrepancy in an interview with Amnesty International, the military prosecutor said that he had information that some bodies had been directly handed to families by hospitals, and that he was investigating this.\n[127]\nA confidential UN Joint Human Rights Office (UNJRO) report, seen by Amnesty International, put the death toll at 102 dead, including 90 men, eight women and four boys, as of 11 October.\n[128]\nWhat the wounds tell about the mass shootings\nAmnesty International reviewed medical reports from three Goma hospitals about these events, covering 84 cases of gunshot wounds, including 28 with brief descriptions of the wounds.\n[129]\nThey suggest that soldiers aimed for vital parts of victims’ bodies, hitting them in the head, arms, abdomen, thorax and back. Heal Africa, a local hospital specializing in the treatment of serious injuries, admitted seven of the most seriously wounded who were transferred from the Goma Military Hospital.\n[130]\nIts report mentions the case of Mathene Busala, a pregnant woman of an undetermined age who was admitted with multiple firearm wounds, including a haemothorax (accumulation of blood within the pleural cavity), open injury to the abdomen, and fracture of the pelvis. “She just passed away during the writing of this report,” the report says.\n[131]\n\nAnother case was that of Muhindo Buroko, a 53-year-old man admitted to intensive care at Heal Africa Hospital with “open trauma to the chest by firearm with massive haemothorax.”\n[132]\nBy the evening of 1 September, hospitals had confirmed nine deaths due to injuries and had three victims in intensive care.\n[133]\nAmnesty International also spoke with relatives of five out of ten people who were reportedly unaccounted for since 30 August, in connection with these events.\n[134]\nAmong them is Antoinette (family name withheld for security reasons) aged 38, along with her four-year-old daughter Francine. Antoinette’s sister-in-law told Amnesty International: “On the morning of 29 August, [Antoinette] arrived in Goma with her baby by boat from the village of Buhozi, to attend the Wazalendo celebration that was scheduled for 30 August 2023. I personally went to see her at the port because she had told me by phone that she had a parcel of food from the village for me. After handing me the parcel, she left for the temple, promising to come and visit my family after their feast day. She never came back and never returned to the village. We went around morgues, detention centres and hospitals, but never found her.”\n[135]\nWamba is Antoinette’s older brother. He said: “She was the mother of four, and her husband died not long ago. Until now I don’t know what to tell my little nephews. The family doesn’t know how to mourn. The authorities won’t tell us anything.”\n[136]\nAnother relative of a missing person said:\n“My 20-year-old brother-in-law Rodrigue Busimwa lived with me. He was a fervent member of the Wazalendo and even sang in one of their choirs. He had been spending the night at their temple for four days to prepare for their big celebration on 30 August. But since then, he has never returned home. We could not find him among those detained, or among the wounded in hospitals, or even among the dead.”\n[137]\n28-year-old Shabantu Kasisi was one of the Wazalendo followers delegated to talk with army officers, and who, according to several witnesses, was among the first to be shot dead in the mass shooting. His sister and two eyewitnesses unequivocally identified him in one video linked to the shooting at the Wazalendo temple.\n[138]\nPointing to a man wearing a black t-shirt and peacefully engaging with soldiers, the sister told Amnesty International: “It is him. You can see how he was calming other protesters. Why did they kill him? What happened to his body? I have so many questions, but will anybody ever answer them? Up to now, our mother has refused to accept that Shabantu is dead until she sees his body with her own eyes. I am afraid she could die of anguish.”\n[139]\nRelatives identified Shabantu Kasisi talking to security officers in videos filmed on the day of the massacre. He has been missing since then.\nThe wife of another missing person said that she saw her husband’s body being dragged by soldiers in the street after the shooting in one of the videos. She explained that they visited all morgues in Goma trying to find his body, to no avail.\n[140]\nAccording to one of their lawyers, authorities did little to nothing to address claims about missing persons and bodies during the soldiers’ trial.\n[141]\nBodies Left to Rot\nAccording to the North Kivu Military Court, 43 victims’ bodies were taken to the military hospital morgue on 30 August. Eyewitnesses told Amnesty International that at least 42 bodies were stacked and wrapped in tarpaulins, where they remained for at least two weeks, regardless of whether it was sunny or raining.\n[142]\n\nOne morgue employee said:\n“The hospital’s morgue has a capacity of only 15 people, and it was already full. All we could do was to embalm them regularly with formaldehyde and hope that they would be buried quickly.”\n[143]\nAt first, military authorities prevented relatives of those killed from seeing the bodies and trying to identify their loved ones, and they were not permitted to take them for mourning and family burial. One man who managed to see the bodies on 30 August thanks to a family acquaintance in the army described the situation:\n“The bodies were piled one on top of the other on a tarpaulin, like garbage bags. Two morgue employees helped remove the bodies from above and placed them on the side. I recognized my nephew by the red t-shirt and the blue jeans he was wearing. A bullet had pierced his back and exploded his chest. His face was wrinkled but intact. I thanked them and left to inform the family so that we could start the mourning”.\n[144]\nNearly two weeks after the massacre, authorities finally allowed families to identify their loved ones.\n[145]\nBy then, it was too late, as they were in putrefaction because they had not been properly preserved. Four people who saw the bodies told Amnesty International that the smell was unbearable and identification almost impossible.\nOne lawyer who had accompanied one of his clients for identification said: “They had bloated; the skin had turned white, just getting close to it was unbearable.”\n[146]\nThe man who had been fortunate enough to see his nephew’s body on 30 August had to return and try to identify his nephew because his body had not been tagged. He said:\n“This time, the bodies were wrapped in individual mortuary bags and placed in rows on the tarpaulins. Morgue employees took people around, opening one bag after another until you identified your relative. The smell was unbearable, but I plucked up the courage and took it in stride. Jean-Claude was no longer the same. I only recognized him by the trim of his beard and his belt. The rest was totally unrecognizable. I broke down in tears.”\n[147]\nMany relatives of those killed were denied the right to identify and bury their loved ones with dignity and according to their faith. Bodies of those killed were dragged on the ground, unceremoniously piled onto a truck, and then left to rot in a hospital yard for three weeks, much to the chagrin of their families.\nUndignified Burials\nSeveral attempts by the Wazalendo and civil society groups in Goma to hold public mourning ceremonies were banned or violently dispersed. For example, on 1 September, the police dispersed a public mourning ceremony held near Stade de l’Unité by beating mourners with sticks and firing tear gas, injuring at least two participants, and arbitrarily arresting activist Josué Kabanza.\n[148]\nHe was released without charge on 4 September, following protests by other activists.\n[149]\nThe government decided to proceed with a burial on 18 September for the 56 people whose deaths had been officially recorded.\n[150]\nSix victims’ relatives told Amnesty International that they were not consulted or informed in advance. One man whose brother was among those killed and identified said: “On Sunday evening, I listened to a communiqué from the Mayor of Goma on the radio, asking families to go to the military hospital the next day in the morning for the burial of the Wazalendo. They did not even dare to call or send us an SMS. There are family members who had wished to attend but could not because they did not have the information in advance.”\n[151]\nThose detained were not given a chance to mourn their loved ones, despite their request through their lawyers.", "excerpt_sha256": "b57833c206f3e093a964d7f2cc4cd4d87745274372006512c6a889ce4c23cdae", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0011-E021", "source_id": "SRC-1fe7819e54f39ddf", "url": "https://www.amnesty.org/en/latest/research/2024/12/drc-operation-keba", "title": "Massacre by the Army in the DRC", "publisher": "Amnesty International", "raw_sha256": "d1c4f240dbfdc5208b9ada0ebe384b4cb3322e5642314b4feb73fad2ef0d96c5", "fact_summary": "Military authorities prosecuted 116 Wazalendo followers and convicted 64, including four sentenced to death, while the separate trial of security personnel convicted one commander and three soldiers. Amnesty found the prosecutions of civilians lacked individualized evidence, the investigations were incomplete, victims and defense witnesses were restricted, and the court recognized only 109 victims and ordered USD 100,000 in reparations.", "question_ids": ["3B.4", "3B.5", "3B.8", "3B.9", "4B.4", "7B.2", "7B.7"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "The arrests, trials, convictions, and reparations order occurred between September and December 2023.", "ruler_attribution": "The proceedings were conducted by state military authorities; the source does not establish Tshisekedi's direct role in the judicial decisions.", "limitations": ["Amnesty characterizes the proceedings as flawed while acknowledging convictions of some security personnel.", "Appeals were pending at the time of the report."], "start_unit": 22, "end_unit": 25, "locator": "extracted HTML block 22 through extracted HTML block 25", "exact_excerpt": "AFP via Getty Images\nJustice… What justice! All this was nothing but a PR stunt to dissipate pressure and to be able to tell anyone who brings up the topic: the officer responsible was punished and justice was served.\nA lawyer for the victims\nSurvivors in the Dock\nOn 30 August, as authorities congratulated themselves on “foiling a plot against Goma”, they announced the arrest of 158 people whom they described as “armed bandits, drugged, in the pay of M23 and Rwandan terrorists.”\n[172]\nTwo days later, 13 of them were released, while 145 others, including 29 children, were brought before the Garrison Military Court of Goma and charged with “participation in an insurrectional movement”, “criminal conspiracy”, and “murder.”\n[173]\nThe 29 children were transferred to the juvenile justice system, which dropped charges and released them in mid-September. Meanwhile, 116 adults, including 102 men and 14 women, remained in detention as they were brought to trial.\n[174]\nOn 9 October, the Garrison Military Court of Goma found 64 of them guilty of “participation in insurrectional movement”, “murder” and “attempted murder” of police officers. The court sentenced Efraimu Bisimwa and three other Wazalendo leaders to death, while 60 followers were sentenced to 10 to 20 years in prison.\n[175]\nThe court acquitted 52 people who were subsequently freed from prison.\n[176]\nThe court prosecuted the 116 Wazalendo followers, 64 of whom it convicted of “participation in insurrectional movement” solely because they belonged to the religious and political group or because they planned or participated in the 30 August protest, which was meant to be peaceful. The court ruled that spending the night at the Wazalendo temple when the 30 August protest had been banned by the mayor of Goma was proof of participation in an insurrectional movement.\n[177]\nYet, in the separate trial of the officers and soldiers for killing the protesters, the North Kivu Military Court dismissed the officers’ claims that the Wazalendo were armed or used the gun they took from a police officer, thereby rejecting that there was any armed resistance or actions that could constitute insurrection.\n[178]\nRegarding the charges of “criminal participation” in the murder of a police officer, the Garrison Court of Goma’s judgment did not show how each of the 64 convicts participated in the stoning of the police officer nor provided any evidence whatsoever in this regard.\n[179]\nNot all these people, if any, could have individually participated in the stoning of the police officer. Therefore, for at least some of these people, their sole crime was to be or to be perceived to be members of the Wazalendo, to have participated in the protest and/or to have found themselves in the path of soldiers during the crackdown. A resident of Nyabushongo who witnessed intimidation and arbitrary arrests by Special Forces soldiers in his neighbourhood after the massacre told Amnesty International: “After the mass shooting, the Hiboux [Special Forces soldiers] went into the shops and houses in the neighbourhood, confiscating smartphones and arresting people, especially men. It was as if, for them, everyone in the neighbourhood was Wazalendo, and that was a crime.\n[180]\nI saw them arrest four young men in a shop. They released three of them, but they took the fourth away, and he is still in prison.”\n[181]\nThis was corroborated by the Garrison Military Court of Goma, which found that at least 52 out of the 116 people it prosecuted had been arrested by soldiers outside of any protest. This included cases where individuals were apprehended in their homes and shops.\n\n[182]\nDespite this, the court alleged that while many may not have directly participated in the stoning of the police officer, they must have encouraged and approved of it by their presence at the crime scene and were, therefore, guilty.\n[183]\nAs for the “attempted murder” conviction, the court relied solely on the testimonies of three police officers who claimed that they had been attacked and wounded by a group of Wazalendo followers while on foot patrol, in the incident in which their colleague was kidnapped, taken to the Wazalendo temple, and later stoned to death.\n[184]\nThey identified two Wazalendo followers whom they said were part of the group which attacked them.\n[185]\nNo further evidence was adduced to support these claims and, above all, to determine the individual responsibility of each of the accused.\nAmnesty International is of the view that the Garrison Military Court of Goma failed to sufficiently demonstrate that the 64 convicts had individually participated in the murder of police officer Kipapa Safari or were even present when he was stoned. In addition, civilians should never be tried by military courts.\n[186]\nThey should either be immediately released or retried in conformity with fair trial standards before competent civilian courts and without the recourse to the death penalty.\nThese proceedings against the survivors of the 30 August massacre largely appear to be an attempt by the military justice system to primarily hold the Wazalendo leader and followers responsible for the violence that took place on 30 August in Goma and its consequences, rather than an attempt to genuinely investigate and prosecute those responsible for the stoning of the police officer.\nOne of their lawyers summed up what their prosecution meant to them:\n“These are survivors of carnage. About half of them were bystanders, neighbours, and other people unrelated to the Wazalendo. Many were tortured during or after their arrest. One woman was completely undressed. Those injured were not treated. Many lost a family member in the massacre. Bisimwa himself lost two of his children. Yet they were the ones who were brought to justice, called terrorists, and humiliated. Worse, they are being judged by the army that attacked them, and with the assistance of MONUSCO, against whom they were about to protest. Do you think this qualifies as justice?”\n[187]\nThose convicted appealed, and their case before the North Kivu Military Court started on 29 December 2023. At the time of writing, the appeal trial was still ongoing.\nPerpetrators’ Accountability\nAs the Wazalendo were on trial, many people denounced the authorities’ eagerness to arrest and prosecute survivors of the massacre rather than holding the military accountable.\n[188]\nIt was not until five days after the massacre and because of mounting public pressure that authorities took some steps towards justice and accountability. On 3 September, the government\n[189]\nannounced that six Republican Guards, including the Republican Guard commander for North Kivu and his deputy, had been arrested and would be brought to trial.\n[190]\nIn addition, the government announced that the governor and operations commander for North Kivu, Lieutenant-General Constant Ndima, had been “immediately recalled to Kinshasa for consultations” and that two police officials in Goma were suspended from their duties and also recalled to Kinshasa.\n[191]\n\nIn a press release published after the killings in Goma, MONUSCO said it “remains concerned by the threats of violence made prior to the demonstration.”\nThe trial of the two officers and four Republican Guard soldiers was held before the North Kivu Military Court in Goma from 4 September to 3 October 2023. They were charged with crimes against humanity by murder and violating orders.\n[192]\nOn 2 October, the court handed down its judgment. The commander of the Republican Guard in North Kivu, Colonel Mike Kalamba Mikombe, was found guilty and sentenced to death for “murder”, “attempted murder”, and “destruction of armaments.” He was also dismissed from the armed forces.\n[193]\nThe Special Forces Regiment Commander and one soldier were acquitted, while three other soldiers were sentenced to 10 years in prison for “murder” and “attempted murder.”\n[194]\nIn addition, the court recognized 109 people as victims and ordered Mike Mikombe and the three soldiers to pay them the equivalent of 100,000 US dollars for reparations\n[195]\n(917 US dollars per person on average). However, the court did not recognize the FNJMN/Wazalendo as a legal entity capable of bringing claims before a court of law, and as a victim.\n[196]\nThe court also rejected Efraimu Bisimwa’s claim for reparations on the grounds of “immorality.”\n[197]\nThe investigations carried out by the authorities on both the murder of the police officer and the unlawful killings of the Wazalendo followers were marred by serious shortcomings:\n[198]\nNo investigators were sent to the shooting scenes to collect evidence.\n[199]\nBy the military prosecutor’s own admission, there were no ballistic investigations or autopsies (while the DRC has limited capabilities in this area, a UN official told Amnesty International that they expressed readiness to provide technical assistance, but the authorities did not seem interested in the offer).\n[200]\nThe military prosecutor said: “We did not request UN assistance for autopsies, but we did for ballistic investigation. I think they sent an expert from Kinshasa – I caught a glimpse of him here – but unfortunately, I had a lot of setbacks, and we ended up not meeting.”\n[201]\nAmnesty International believes the investigation failed to identify all those who should have been investigated for their possible responsibility.\nThe investigation also failed to identify the names, ages, genders, addresses and family members of all 56 officially recognized dead. Moreover, some victims are still missing. These gaps in the investigation prevented several families from participating in the trial to get justice for their relatives or to request reparations.\nThroughout the trial of the Republican Guard officers and soldiers, only six victims were called to testify, compared to 33 requested by victims’ lawyers.\n[202]\n\nOne lawyer representing the victims stated that “each witness would potentially bring some new information or a new perspective in the interests of truth and justice, but after hearing from only six witnesses, the court said it was ‘sufficiently enlightened’ and refused to call any more witnesses”.\n[203]\nWhile victims appealed the court’s judgment in the case of six members of the Republican Guard, they were not able to effectively exercise their appeal rights due to the remoteness of the appeal court (the Superior Military Court), which sits 2000km away, in the capital Kinshasa.\n[204]\nVictims and their lead counsel told Amnesty International that they may not be able to travel to Kinshasa to participate in the appeal trial and expressed concerns that the injustices they have experienced may never be addressed.\n[205]\nOn 25 October 2023, the victims’ committee wrote to the president of the military appeals court, formally requesting that the court hold the appeal hearings in Goma on an exceptional basis to ensure their effectiveness.\n[206]\nThey were yet to receive a response at the time of writing,\n[207]\nwhile the appeal trial continued in Kinshasa.\nRestriction on defence rights in the trial of the army officers and soldiers: of the 12 witnesses called by the defence, only three appeared before the court.\n[208]\nAt least three times, the court rejected requests from the victims’ and defence lawyers to summon Governor Constant Ndima to testify before the court, even as a witness.\n[209]\nOne defence counsel in the Wazalendo lawsuit commented: “Justice… What justice! All this was nothing but a PR stunt to dissipate pressure and to be able to tell anyone who brings up the topic: the officer responsible was punished, and justice served. In the meantime, many families still don’t know the whereabouts of their loved ones, a critical radio station has been silenced, a church has been reduced to ashes, and dozens of innocent people are languishing in prison.”\n[210]\nback to top\nLegal analysis\nAFP via Getty Images\nPeople have a right to express themselves freely and to assemble peacefully, even if in protest at the United Nations and other actors. Authorities must facilitate the right to peaceful assembly.\nSpokesperson for the UN High Commissioner for Human Rights,\nRavina Shamdasani, commenting on the army and police massacre on 30 August 2023 in Goma – 1 September 2023\nCongolese authorities must respect, protect and fulfil human rights at all times, whether during an armed conflict or in peacetime. Notwithstanding the public declaration of a so-called “State of Siege”, introduced in North Kivu and Ituri provinces in May 2021, States Parties are only permitted to derogate from certain provisions of the International Covenant on Civil and Political Rights if the proclamation of the state of emergency meets certain requirements, including that the circumstances are notified in writing to the UN Secretary-General\n[211]", "excerpt_sha256": "c1947118893dd039129564f66e287ebb4300a8e2ef865231e934751e042adfae", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0012-R03-E001", "source_id": "SRC-34b3eac040af6ecf", "url": "https://www.amnesty.org/en/latest/news/2023/05/drc-authorities-must-end-state-of-siege", "title": "DRC: Authorities must lift ‘state of siege’ now.", "publisher": "Amnesty International", "raw_sha256": "9b7b12d11b1e91bed5f9520ded628516b0dedd35a00dd33aa12a280ee4f1d818", "fact_summary": "The state of siege in Nord-Kivu and Ituri was extended more than 50 times by the government without meaningful public debate about the legality or merits of those extensions.", "question_ids": ["3B.5", "4B.4", "4B.6"], "polarity": "adverse", "period_fit": "The extensions and their assessment were documented during 2023, within the target period.", "ruler_attribution": "The source attributes the repeated extensions to the government under President Félix Tshisekedi.", "limitations": ["Amnesty International is the reporting organization and characterizes the extensions as unlawful; le 12/10/2022\nEtablissement(s) :\nUniversité Paris-Panthéon-Assas\nEcole(s) doctorale(s) :\nEGIC Economie, gestion, information et communication\nPartenaire(s) de recherche :\nLaboratoire :\nCentre d'analyse et de recherche interdisciplinaires sur les médias\nMots clés\nFR\n|\nEN\nMots clés libres\nStratégies discursives\nDiscours politique\nRhétorique politique\nRésumé\nFR\n|\nEN\nCette thèse examine les stratégies discursives utilisées par Félix-Antoine Tshisekedi, Président de la République Démocratique du Congo, dans ses discours sur l'état de la Nation de 2018 à 2023. En s'appuyant sur l'analyse critique du discours (Fairclough, Van Dijk), l'étude explore comment ces discours sont délibérément conçus pour légitimer l'autorité du Président, renforcer son pouvoir et mobiliser l'opinion publique dans un contexte politique instable. Trois dimensions sont analysées : les techniques de persuasion, les thèmes récurrents comme la sécurité et l'économie, ainsi que l'adaptation des discours aux changements sociaux et politiques. Cette recherche met en lumière les dynamiques rhétoriques à travers lesquelles le langage politique devient un outil stratégique de gouvernance, influençant les perceptions et les comportements politiques en RDC. Elle offre également une contribution originale à l'étude du discours politique en Afrique, en analysant des discours inédits dans la recherche scientifique.\ntheses.fr\nLe moteur de recherche theses.fr recense l’ensemble des thèses de doctorat soutenues en France depuis 1985, les sujets de thèse préparés au sein des établissements de l’enseignement supérieur français, et les personnes impliquées dans la recherche doctorale française. La base de données est en constante évolution.\nEn savoir plus\nRessources", "excerpt_sha256": "63ac9d7f0a0b0093b988ff53d7cf7e8aa255be27a54bfa0c55f23b573209c3a5", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": ["The excerpt explicitly describes the speeches as deliberately designed to legitimate presidential authority, reinforce presidential power, and mobilize public opinion in an unstable political context."]}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0013-E008", "source_id": "SRC-2085e4ebd4fd46c1", "url": "https://www.issafrica.org/iss-today/drc-misses-another-opportunity-to-build-a-democracy", "title": "DRC misses another opportunity to build a democracy", "publisher": "Institute for Security Studies", "raw_sha256": "d8685a5db741dded567a529efb0ad00261eb29b4d107c4540f8775d1b21e5ce1", "fact_summary": "CENI declared Tshisekedi the winner of the December 2023 election with over 70% of the vote, but opposition candidates and citizens disputed the result, and observers documented 5,402 significant polling-station irregularities, late opening at two-thirds of stations, and procedural compliance at only 57% of stations.", "question_ids": ["4B.1", "4B.10", "4B.2", "4B.4", "4B.6", "7B.1", "7B.2", "7B.7", "8B.10", "8B.9"], "polarity": "adverse", "period_fit": "The election occurred in December 2023; the assessment was published in February 2024 and reports contemporaneous findings.", "ruler_attribution": "The irregularities concern the election administered under Tshisekedi's presidency; the supplied source does not prove that he personally ordered specific irregularities.", "limitations": ["The source reports allegations and observer findings rather than a final judicial determination.", "The article was published after the target period."], "start_unit": 3, "end_unit": 4, "locator": "extracted HTML block 3 through extracted HTML block 4", "exact_excerpt": "ISS Today\nBy\nNirvaly Mooloo\nResearch Officer, Central Africa and Lake Chad Basin, ISS\nRemadji Hoinathy\nSenior Researcher, Central Africa and Lake Chad Basin, ISS\nFollowing President Félix Tshisekedi’s contested victory in the Democratic Republic of the Congo’s (DRC) December 2023 elections, democracy hangs in the balance. Amid allegations of irregularities and voter disenfranchisement, Tshisekedi’s re-election underscores deep problems with the country’s democratic fabric.\nThe Independent National Electoral Commission (CENI) declared Tshisekedi the winner with over 70% of the\nvotes\n. He was followed by Moïse Katumbi with 18%, Martin Fayulu with 5% and Denis Mukwege with less than 1%.\nMany believe the president’s landslide victory is implausible. Opposition candidates and citizens\nview\nthe country’s synchronised presidential, local, provincial and national polls as a ‘farce’, and calls have been made for a rerun.\nPre-election predictions conducted by\nGeoPoll\nsuggested that Tshisekedi would win on the basis of limited socio-economic improvements such as free education, and a divided opposition. But the extent of his landslide victory had raised questions about the whole process.\nThe national electoral commission didn’t provide a voter register for review and verification\nThe international community, opposition and civil society groups continuously warned about\ndeficiencies\nin CENI’s pre-election preparations. An observer mission of the Catholic Church and Church of Christ in the Congo documented 5 402 significant irregularities at polling stations, such as malfunctioning voting devices, unopened polling stations and ballot stuffing. Site de la Misson d’Observation Électorale (SYMOCEL), a domestic observer group,\nreported\nthat two-thirds of polling stations opened late, and only 57% complied with procedures.\nMoreover, despite CENI announcing that 44 million people (of the total 100 million) had registered to vote, no voter register was provided for review and verification. CENI had reportedly\ndeclined\noffers from the African Union (AU) and United Nations (UN) to audit the electoral roll. Instead, an auditor\n\nreviewed\nthe figures over five days. CENI also failed to increase transparency during voter registration and rejected the opposition National Episcopal Conference of the Congo’s calls for an independent audit of the voter registry.\nThe 2023 elections exhibit minimal progress from the pivotal 2018 polls, which represented the DRC’s first chance for a democratic transfer of power. Both elections have been tainted by similar allegations.\nIn 2018, both the Catholic Church and SYMOCEL reported fraud and irregularities, including being prevented from observing, missing ballots and missing voting machines. Discrepancies arose between tallies of local results compilation centres and polling stations, resulting in the former refusing to endorse the official count.\nFew of SADC’s recommendations for electoral reform were implemented by CENI or the government\nThe AU, UN and Southern African Development Community (SADC) also noted problems with the 2018 polls, with\nSADC\ncalling on Kinshasa to recount the votes. Despite all these issues, the three regional organisations said overall, the election went well. So it’s probably unsurprising that few of the SADC observer mission’s recommendations for electoral reform were implemented by CENI or the government before December’s polls.\nIn the 2023 elections, the international community has again overlooked serious electoral flaws. The UN\ncalled\nfor a probe only into reports of hate speech and inciting violence, and the AU and SADC haven’t taken a definitive position.\nDuring the 2018 elections, Fayulu emerged as a prominent opposition figure, contesting Tshisekedi’s win. Fayulu\nlabelled\nthe contested poll an ‘electoral coup,’ accusing Tshisekedi of striking a deal with outgoing president Joseph Kabila. This sentiment has also tainted the 2023 polls, with opposition candidates calling for joint protests.\nPersistent allegations of irregularities, fraud and political manipulation in 2018 and 2023 have damaged public trust in the electoral process and eroded the credibility of democratic institutions across the DRC. The country desperately needs independent electoral commissions and judicial institutions.\nLike in 2018, the international community has again overlooked serious electoral problems\nThe problem of\nnon-voters\nwill also have far-reaching implications for democracy. The resurgence of M23 and the hundred or so armed groups in east DRC, notably the Wazalendo coalition, meant that voting didn’t happen in Rutshuru and Masisi territories. This disenfranchisement of hundreds of thousands of citizens also marred the 2018 elections and could fuel civil unrest and instability.\nThe government must remain receptive to dialogue with the political opposition on reforming the electoral process. This will be pivotal to quelling tensions and allowing the opposition to participate in governing the nation. Security in Rutshuru and Masisi must also be bolstered, although current dynamics suggest this will take some time.\nTshisekedi must collaborate with CENI to implement reforms that guarantee integrity, transparency and inclusivity in future elections. Without that, public trust in the electoral process and democratic institutions will remain low, with damaging consequences for the DRC’s stability. Amid allegations of irregularities and voter disenfranchisement, Tshisekedi’s re-election underscores deep problems with the country’s democratic fabric.\nThe Independent National Electoral Commission (CENI) declared Tshisekedi the winner with over 70% of the\nvotes\n. He was followed by Moïse Katumbi with 18%, Martin Fayulu with 5% and Denis Mukwege with less than 1%.\nMany believe the president’s landslide victory is implausible. Opposition candidates and citizens\nview\nthe country’s synchronised presidential, local, provincial and national polls as a ‘farce’, and calls have been made for a rerun.\nPre-election predictions conducted by\nGeoPoll\nsuggested that Tshisekedi would win on the basis of limited socio-economic improvements such as free education, and a divided opposition. But the extent of his landslide victory had raised questions about the whole process.\nThe national electoral commission didn’t provide a voter register for review and verification\nThe international community, opposition and civil society groups continuously warned about\ndeficiencies\nin CENI’s pre-election preparations. An observer mission of the Catholic Church and Church of Christ in the Congo documented 5 402 significant irregularities at polling stations, such as malfunctioning voting devices, unopened polling stations and ballot stuffing. Site de la Misson d’Observation Électorale (SYMOCEL), a domestic observer group,\nreported\nthat two-thirds of polling stations opened late, and only 57% complied with procedures.\nMoreover, despite CENI announcing that 44 million people (of the total 100 million) had registered to vote, no voter register was provided for review and verification. CENI had reportedly\ndeclined\noffers from the African Union (AU) and United Nations (UN) to audit the electoral roll. Instead, an auditor\n\nreviewed\nthe figures over five days. CENI also failed to increase transparency during voter registration and rejected the opposition National Episcopal Conference of the Congo’s calls for an independent audit of the voter registry.\nThe 2023 elections exhibit minimal progress from the pivotal 2018 polls, which represented the DRC’s first chance for a democratic transfer of power. Both elections have been tainted by similar allegations.\nIn 2018, both the Catholic Church and SYMOCEL reported fraud and irregularities, including being prevented from observing, missing ballots and missing voting machines. Discrepancies arose between tallies of local results compilation centres and polling stations, resulting in the former refusing to endorse the official count.\nFew of SADC’s recommendations for electoral reform were implemented by CENI or the government\nThe AU, UN and Southern African Development Community (SADC) also noted problems with the 2018 polls, with\nSADC\ncalling on Kinshasa to recount the votes. Despite all these issues, the three regional organisations said overall, the election went well. So it’s probably unsurprising that few of the SADC observer mission’s recommendations for electoral reform were implemented by CENI or the government before December’s polls.\nIn the 2023 elections, the international community has again overlooked serious electoral flaws. The UN\ncalled\nfor a probe only into reports of hate speech and inciting violence, and the AU and SADC haven’t taken a definitive position.\nDuring the 2018 elections, Fayulu emerged as a prominent opposition figure, contesting Tshisekedi’s win. Fayulu\nlabelled\nthe contested poll an ‘electoral coup,’ accusing Tshisekedi of striking a deal with outgoing president Joseph Kabila. This sentiment has also tainted the 2023 polls, with opposition candidates calling for joint protests.\nPersistent allegations of irregularities, fraud and political manipulation in 2018 and 2023 have damaged public trust in the electoral process and eroded the credibility of democratic institutions across the DRC. The country desperately needs independent electoral commissions and judicial institutions.\nLike in 2018, the international community has again overlooked serious electoral problems\nThe problem of\nnon-voters\nwill also have far-reaching implications for democracy. The resurgence of M23 and the hundred or so armed groups in east DRC, notably the Wazalendo coalition, meant that voting didn’t happen in Rutshuru and Masisi territories. This disenfranchisement of hundreds of thousands of citizens also marred the 2018 elections and could fuel civil unrest and instability.\nThe government must remain receptive to dialogue with the political opposition on reforming the electoral process. This will be pivotal to quelling tensions and allowing the opposition to participate in governing the nation. Security in Rutshuru and Masisi must also be bolstered, although current dynamics suggest this will take some time.\nTshisekedi must collaborate with CENI to implement reforms that guarantee integrity, transparency and inclusivity in future elections. Without that, public trust in the electoral process and democratic institutions will remain low, with damaging consequences for the DRC’s stability. SADC, the AU and UN can provide support and expertise in this regard.\nExclusive rights to re-publish ISS Today articles have been given to\nDaily Maverick\nin South Africa and\nPremium Times", "excerpt_sha256": "2a6015557be1af8f8d46671b4c2be59cd9221e4b823b746b530590ff3d1e4ae5", "verification_status": "corrected", "verification_notes": ["The excerpt says CENI had reportedly declined the AU and UN offers, so the attribution is narrowed accordingly.", "The excerpt says an auditor reviewed the figures over five days; it does not explicitly call this an internal audit in the supplied English passage."]}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0013-E010", "source_id": "SRC-2085e4ebd4fd46c1", "url": "https://www.issafrica.org/iss-today/drc-misses-another-opportunity-to-build-a-democracy", "title": "DRC misses another opportunity to build a democracy", "publisher": "Institute for Security Studies", "raw_sha256": "d8685a5db741dded567a529efb0ad00261eb29b4d107c4540f8775d1b21e5ce1", "fact_summary": "The article reported that few SADC electoral-reform recommendations from the 2018 elections were implemented by CENI or the government before the December 2023 polls, and that persistent allegations of irregularity, fraud and political manipulation in 2018 and 2023 eroded trust in electoral processes and democratic institutions.", "question_ids": ["4B.10", "4B.4", "4B.6", "7B.10", "7B.2", "7B.6", "8B.10", "8B.8"], "polarity": "adverse", "period_fit": "The reported lack of reform implementation is assessed before the December 2023 election.", "ruler_attribution": "The source attributes nonimplementation to CENI or the government collectively; it does not isolate Tshisekedi's personal responsibility.", "limitations": ["The source does not list which recommendations were or were not implemented.", "The article is a post-election assessment."], "start_unit": 4, "end_unit": 4, "locator": "extracted HTML block 4", "exact_excerpt": "reviewed\nthe figures over five days. hauts magistrats nouvellement nommés ont prêté serment devant le Chef de l’état\n69 hauts magistrats de la Cour de cassation, du Conseil d’État, et des parquets généraux près ces hautes juridictions ont prêté serment, ce jeudi, devant le Président Félix Tshisekedi.\nC’était au cours d’une cérémonie organisée au Palais de la Nation.\nCes hauts magistrats, nommés mi-août dernier, ont juré « de respecter la Constitution et les lois de la République Démocratique du Congo (RDC), de remplir loyalement et fidèlement, avec honneur et dignité, les fonctions qui leur sont confiées ». Ils ont ensuite signé le procès-verbal de prestation de serment.\nLe Président de la République, en sa qualité de Magistrat suprême, a pris acte de leur serment.\nAprès ainsi s’être acquittés de cette obligation constitutionnelle, ces hauts magistrats entrent officiellement en fonction dès ce jour. Il s’agit précisément d’1 premier président, 8 présidents et 20 conseillers de la Cour de cassation, 5 premiers avocats-généraux de la République et 8 avocats-généraux de la République près la Cour de cassation, 5 présidents et 9 conseillers du Conseil d’État, côté siège, et 5 premiers avocats-généraux et 8 avocats-généraux pour le parquet près le Conseil d'État.\nAu terme d’une série d’ordonnances rendue publique le 15 août dernier, le Chef de l’État avait procédé à de nouvelles mises en place au sein de la magistrature. Nous avons cette mission à remplir jusqu'au sacrifice suprême\", a dit le Chef de l’État.\nLe Garant de le nation a réitéré son engagement à ne ménager aucun effort pour améliorer les conditions de vie des militaires et de leur famille. \"Je compte sur vous pour que vous soyez des femmes et des hommes responsables. Restez toujours fidèles à votre mission. Vous êtes là pour la nation et non pour votre propre intérêt. Le gouvernement mettra tous les moyens pour vous honorer pendant et après votre carrière\", a déclaré le Chef de l'État.\nLe Président de la République a rappelé la place de choix qu’occupent les Forces de défense et de sécurité dans la marche du pays : « vous n’êtes pas n’importe qui, vous êtes les porteurs de cette nation, vous donnez votre vie pour sauver ce pays. »\nA propos de l'insécurité sans l’Est du pays, le Chef de l'État a indiqué que le pays est dans une phase délicate. \"C'est moi votre Commandant suprême, il n’y a rien qui arrivera. Ensemble, nous allons gagner ce combat. \", a rassuré le Président Félix Tshisekedi.\nPrenant la parole pour le compte des FARDC, le Général Ekenge a renouvelé leur serment de loyauté. \"Nous tenons à réitérer notre engagement citoyen de servir la patrie\", a-t-il dit. \" Nous vous assurons de veiller à ce que la paix règne sur toute l'étendue du pays, et de faire échec à toute tentative de déstabilisation du Congo \", a-t-il rajouté.\nCes échanges se sont déroulés en présence notamment du vice-Premier ministre, ministre de la Défense nationale Jean-Pierre Bemba Gombo. La cérémonie s’est clôturée par une photo de famille .\nlire la suite\nPartager cet article\nLes autres unes", "excerpt_sha256": "d3bc3f4ae033915d6280c75ddcf5f8d0c34ce79209dd389eae183ef5d9ed4f4d", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0019-E013", "source_id": "SRC-0c14ae8bd8790fba", "url": "https://www.cfr.org/articles/history-breeds-skepticism-drcs-electoral-results", "title": "History Breeds Skepticism in the DRC’s Electoral Results", "publisher": "Council on Foreign Relations", "raw_sha256": "93c8ffee5713e44fd91357317deff614ffadc23053376af7e6633a308cb354aa", "fact_summary": "CFR summarized reports of illegible voter cards, missing equipment, intimidation, more than 5,000 incidents reported to Catholic and Protestant church observers, mishandling of sensitive materials by electoral agents, and a non-transparent tabulation process.", "question_ids": ["4B.1", "4B.10", "4B.2", "4B.3", "4B.6", "4B.9"], "polarity": "adverse", "period_fit": "The reported incidents occurred during the December 2023 elections.", "ruler_attribution": "The source attributes operational failures to electoral agents and authorities; it does not directly prove Tshisekedi ordered or personally conducted them.", "limitations": ["The document does not identify which incidents were independently verified or describe corrective action by the president."], "start_unit": 2, "end_unit": 2, "locator": "extracted HTML block 2", "exact_excerpt": "CFR Expert\nRalph Bunche Senior Fellow for Africa Policy Studies\nShare\nDecember’s general elections in the Democratic Republic of Congo unfolded as many had predicted. Election day was so chaotic that it stretched into election days, irregularities abounded, the opposition and observer groups cried foul, and incumbent President Felix Tshisekedi was preliminarily declared the winner (the Constitutional Court is expected to confirm this result on January 10). It is customary to congratulate other countries on the conduct of elections. But with over a billion dollars devoted to such a deeply flawed process, Congolese citizens don’t have much to celebrate.\nWith the opposition divided among multiple candidates, Tshisekedi’s victory certainly seems plausible, and even likely. But those dissatisfied with the electoral results have real grievances to support their complaints. From illegible voters’ cards to missing equipment to outright intimidation, a great deal went wrong. Over five thousand incidents were reported to observers representing the Catholic and Protestant churches while the polls were open. Another civil society group, Symocel,\nfound that\nthat agents of the electoral commission mishandled sensitive materials and failed to provide for a transparent tabulation process. The shambolic process only provided fuel for those who believe that the outcome was fixed.\nHistory supplies even more such fuel. Tshisekedi originally came to power in 2019 as a result of a backroom deal with his predecessor and despite hard evidence\nindicating\nthat a different candidate received the most votes. Foreign powers, including the United States, went along with the charade in the hopes of avoiding even more instability in an already fragile state. At this point, trust in electoral institutions is understandably in very short supply. When there is no reason to have confidence in electoral authorities, the “real” winner is in the eye of any individual beholder. For those who did not support the president’s re-election, the notion that they are stakeholders in a system that holds him and his government accountable for their actions sounds like a fantasy.\nHow does a country with so many urgent problems move forward when the legitimacy of its political leadership continues to be an open question for so many? The country’s compromised judiciary is unlikely to deliver any real means of redress for those complaining about irregularities, and authorities have\nindicated\nthat attempts to address political grievances via protest will be quashed forcefully. Rather than strengthening the bond between citizens’ demands and the governance on offer, the hangover from 2019 and the latest polling leave the country mired in mistrust and adds still more tarnish to the brand of democracy in the region. For a country of more than 110 million people, with natural resources so critical to the rest of the world’s future, it’s a painful missed opportunity.\nInformation\nFrom\nAfrica in Transition\nand\nAfrica Program\nCreative Commons: Some rights reserved.\nThis work is licensed under Creative Commons Attribution-NonCommercial-NoDerivatives 4.0 International (CC BY-NC-ND 4.0) License.\nDemocratic Republic of Congo\nElections and Voting\nSub-Saharan Africa\nPolitical Transitions\nTags", "excerpt_sha256": "725d7bac82bad024039cb5e402182c0d245ff2a998b31cdd4d971c0ba2cabcbd", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0020-E013", "source_id": "SRC-a55a30a56ce8c73e", "url": "https://presidence.cd/actualite-detail/actualite/le_president_felix_tshisekedi_a_assiste_a_la_rentree_judiciaire_2023_2024_de_la_cour_de_cassation", "title": "Le Président Félix Tshisekedi a assisté à la rentrée judiciaire 2023–2024 de la cour de cassation", "publisher": "Présidence RDC", "raw_sha256": "ba4d793f9c0e299a4862f3c9a98a87c21636fda88145542c9d32daeb693abad3", "fact_summary": "At the October 2023 judicial reopening of the Court of Cassation, the national bar requested improved conditions and remuneration for magistrates; premier mandat\nCe mardi, devant le Parlement réuni en congrès, le Chef de l’État Félix Tshisekedi a prononcé son discours annuel sur l’état de la nation. Il dit l’avoir fait avec « émotion » puisqu’il s’agit du dernier exercice de cette nature pour son premier quinquennat qui arrive à terme.\nLe Président de la République a commencé par « saluer la mémoire de nos compatriotes, tombés pour les uns, les armes à la main en s’évertuant à sauvegarder notre intégrité, pour les autres fauchés par les affres de la guerre, la violence, les maladies et divers événements naturels ».\nLe Chef de l'État a ensuite passé en revue les principales réalisations de son mandat. Évoquant les réformes entreprises pour renforcer les capacités de riposte et de dissuasion de l’armée, le Président Tshisekedi a rappelé la nécessité du départ de la MONUSCO. « Il est temps pour notre pays de prendre pleinement son destin en main et devenir le principal acteur de sa propre sécurité et de sa propre stabilité », a-t-il dit.\nPar ailleurs, le Chef de l’État estime que « notre économie en 2023 se porte bien ». « Notre taux de croissance a grimpé de 1,7 % en 2020 à 6,2 % cette année, une preuve de la résilience et de la détermination congolaises face à l’adversité mondiale », a-t-il indiqué. Pour juguler l’inflation, il a annoncé des interventions ciblées sur les prix des denrées essentielles et des politiques monétaires pour stabiliser le Franc congolais.\nLe Président Tshisekedi a également fait le tour des avancées dans d'autres secteurs comme la justice, les infrastructures, mais surtout l’éducation et la santé. Il a indiqué que pour soutenir la gratuité de l’enseignement, qu’il considère comme un « grand succès », le gouvernement a augmenté le budget alloué à l’éducation de 9,1 % de 2021 à 2022 et de 23,9 % de l’année 2022 à 2023. « Toujours pour ce quinquennat, le salaire moyen d’un enseignant est passé de 159 662,67 FC à 408 689,67 FC », a-t-il rajouté.\nEn outre, le Chef de l’État s’est réjoui de l’amélioration de la situation de la liberté de la presse. « En l’espace de quatre ans, nous avons gagné 30 places dans le baromètre mondial de la liberté de la presse de Reporters Sans Frontières (RSF) », a-t-il relevé. Il a tout de même appelé « à plus de responsabilité et de professionnalisme des journalistes qui ont un rôle crucial à jouer en ce moment particulier de l’histoire de notre pays tant dans la couverture de la situation sécuritaire à l’Est que dans celle de la campagne électorale et des prochaines élections.\n« Nous avons donc le choix entre repartir à zéro ou consolider les acquis de cet élan progressif en avançant main dans la main, avec une vision claire et un but commun, vers un avenir où chaque Congolais aura la possibilité de prospérer », a-t-il conclu, s’adressant à toute la nation congolaise, après avoir fait le tour d’horizon de son action à la tête du pays depuis bientôt 5 ans.\nlire la suite\nPartager cet article\nLes autres unes", "excerpt_sha256": "356be82520093d03567147eef7e9367b4e17c234677fdcd671a4c9a9232160a3", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0022-E014", "source_id": "SRC-7d183ffcc155044b", "url": "https://www.radiookapi.net/2023/10/20/actualite/securite/goma-marche-de-protestation-des-jeunes-contre-la-passivite-de-la-force", "title": "Goma : marche de protestation des jeunes contre « la passivité de la force de l’EAC dans la guerre du M23 »", "publisher": "Radio Okapi", "raw_sha256": "e4923aba5be624ec376d2baefed46804b378d6592a6fed50f94d3f8757243952", "fact_summary": "On 19 October 2023, Goma youth organizations held a peaceful march criticizing the perceived passivity of the EAC force in the M23 war; externe réalisé par une mission mixte d'experts internationaux, Le fichier électoral jugé fiable\nAprès un audit externe réalisé par une mission mixte d'experts internationaux, Le fichier électoral jugé fiable\nL'équipe mixte d'experts internationaux dévoile les conclusions des travaux consacrés à l'audit externe du Fichier Électoral.\nMardi 23 mai 2023 |\nDépêches\nL’équipe mixte composée de cinq experts internationaux a rendu, le lundi 22 mai 2023, les conclusions de la mission lui assignée par la Commission Électorale Nationale et Indépendante (CENI) d’audit externe du fichier électoral le déclarant fiable, au regard d’un faisceau d’éléments auxquels elle a eu accès avant de les passer au peigne fin.\nSelon le rapport lu par Mme Janet LOVE, lors de la conférence de presse organisée à l'hôtel Béatrice, en présence des membres de la plénière de la CENI dirigé par Denis KADIMA KAZADI, des représentants des organisations de la société civile, des représentations diplomatiques et des médias nationaux et internationaux.\nImage\n\nLes membres de l'assemblée plénière de la CENI suivent avec attention les conclusions de la mission mixte d'experts internationaux.\nCette mission, qui s’est étendue du 15 au 20 mai 2023, composée de deux groupes linguistiques hétérogènes (anglais et français), était constituée outre Janet LOVE (Afrique du Sud), Muhabi LUFU (Malawi), Joseph TOPANGU, Kabata Kabata KABAMBA et Vianney NZANZU (tous Congolais').\nLa première citée, cheffe de mission, a expliqué la méthodologie du travail utilisée et les normes appliquées basée sur des principes internationaux de gestion, de contrôle et d’observation des élections tel qu’adoptés par l’ECF-SADC (Forum des Commissions électorales de la Communauté de développement de l’Afrique australe).\nPour cette tâche ayant trait à la collecte et à la vérification des informations, a indiqué Mme Janet LOVE, ces experts ont pris en compte le rapport d’audit interne fourni par la Centrale électorale.\nSe fondant sur les directives internationales en la matière de l’Institut International pour la Démocratie et l’Assistance électorale (IDEA) et l’examen des systèmes informatiques appliqués aux élections.\nEn sus, cette équipe a tenu compte du nombre d’enrôlés aux quatre cycles électoraux en République démocratique du Congo.\nEn formulant, au bout du compte, un certain nombre de suggestions et des recommandations afin que la CENI puisse accroître, au fil du temps, la crédibilité et la confiance de la population vis-à-vis d’elle.\nNon sans l’avoir encouragée à intensifier la communication interactive avec toutes les parties prenantes.\nLa porte-parole du groupe est revenue sur l’opération d’enregistrement et d’enrôlement des électeurs ayant débouché sur le nettoyage du fichier électoral via un serveur ultra-puissant qui a éliminé les données non-conformes (des doublons, l'enregistrement des mineurs et les enrôlements multiples, photos de photos, etc.).\nLa partie interactive a permis un échange qui a éclairé la lanterne des journalistes ayant soulevé quelques préoccupations après cet exposé.\nPour sa part, le numéro un de la CENI, Denis KADIMA KAZADI, a exprimé la satisfaction du bureau et des membres de la plénière au regard du travail effectué par cette équipe.\n« Mes impressions sont fondées sur la reconnaissance d’un travail qui a été bien exécuté dans un délai fort réduit par des professionnels. Leurs conclusions nous rassurent, car ils ont estimé que le fichier électoral est fiable. En relevant même que nous avons une solide fondation pour le reste. En tant que Président de cette institution qui a travaillé d’arrache-pied, nous ne pouvons qu’être comblés. La prochaine étape, c’est la publication aujourd’hui des statistiques sur l’enrôlement puis le dépôt de l’avant-projet sur la répartition des sièges ce jour même. À partir de cet instant, nous avons tourné la page de l’enrôlement des électeurs et nous sommes focus maintenant sur la réception et le traitement très prochainement des candidatures. Les élections, c’est pour le 20 décembre 2023 et j’encourage les candidats à prendre cela très au sérieux », a déclaré Denis KADIMA KAZADI.\nPour l’heure, la CENI a enrôlé 43 955 181 électeurs contre 40 371 439 au cycle précédent.\nArticles similaires\n,\nLe Président de la CENI et le VPM en charge de la Fonction Publique s’accordent sur l’élaboration du cadre organique de la CENI\nLundi 27 juillet 2026 |\nDépêches\n,\nLe Président de la CENI et le VPM en charge de la Fonction Publique s’accordent sur l’élaboration du cadre organique de la CENI\nLundi 27 juillet 2026 |\nDépêches\nÉgypte : La CENI partage l’expérience de la RDC sur le vote de la diaspora congolaise\nJeudi 9 juillet 2026 |\nDépêches", "excerpt_sha256": "2cc0759c4b48da4c583288bf14732a8f244cd93c849c995bd9dd7cc43eca99f9", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0023-E017", "source_id": "SRC-3edf64f1400a8240", "url": "https://www.ilo.org/fr/resource/actualites/atelier-de-planification-strategique-pilote-de-linspection-du-travail-dans", "title": "Atelier de planification stratégique pilote de l’inspection du travail dans le cadre du projet de soutien à la promotion des normes du travail en République Démocratique du Congo (SPNT/ RDC)", "publisher": "International Labour Organization", "raw_sha256": "a736b2005dd6c41734c176503c7da5c688e2beaf970aeeb851bdb5a36bed5317", "fact_summary": "A January 2023 strategic-planning workshop for DRC labour inspection trained 11 labour inspectors, including two women and two mining inspectors, conducted two construction-site inspection exercises, and produced a six-month strategic plan and a 2023 action plan with performance indicators.", "question_ids": ["5B.6", "6B.4", "6B.9", "8B.2", "8B.4", "8B.6"], "polarity": "favorable", "period_fit": "Directly concerns institutional capacity-building undertaken in January 2023.", "ruler_attribution": "The workshop was conducted under the patronage of the labour ministry with ILO support; it is not directly attributed to Tshisekedi personally.", "limitations": ["The source does not report whether the plan was funded, implemented, or effective after the workshop."], "start_unit": 2, "end_unit": 3, "locator": "extracted HTML block 2 through extracted HTML block 3", "exact_excerpt": "Fermer\nPlus\nNavigation secondaire\nActualités\nRéunions et événements\nConference internationale du Travail\nConseil d'Administration\nIndustries et secteurs\nDirecteur général de l'OIT\nBureau des activités des employeurs (ACT/EMP)\nBureau des activités pour les travailleurs (ACTRAV)\nILOSTAT\nILO Live\nLa réforme de l'OIT\nPhotos\nContactez nous\nSort by\nLe plus pertinent\nLe plus récent\nActualités\nCOMMUNIQUE DE PRESSE - PROJET DRC/21/03/USA – SPNT\nAtelier de planification stratégique pilote de l’inspection du travail dans le cadre du projet de soutien à la promotion des normes du travail en République Démocratique du Congo (SPNT/ RDC)\nSous le patronage du Ministère de l’Emploi, Travail et Prévoyance Sociale, en collaboration avec l’Organisation Internationale du Travail (OIT), l’atelier de planification stratégique pilote de l’inspection du travail de la République Démocratique du Congo s’est tenu du 09 au 20 janvier 2023 dans le cadre du projet SPNT/RDC. Ce projet d’une durée de 40 mois, financé par le Gouvernement américain à travers son Ministère du Travail (USDOL) a pour objectif d’apporter un appui technique au Gouvernement de la RDC et aux partenaires sociaux pour progresser sur les questions de respect des normes du travail.\n31 janvier 2023\nFacebook\n\nX\nLinkedin\nContenu également disponible en:\nEnglish\nLa cérémonie d’ouverture de cet atelier résidentiel a eu lieu à CARITAS-Congo, dans la Commune de la Gombe, en présence de représentants de l’Inspection Générale du Travail, des tribunaux du travail et des organisations d’employeurs et de travailleurs ainsi que des personnalités de référence dans le domaine de l’Inspection du travail.\nCet atelier, regroupant 11 inspecteurs du travail dont deux femmes et deux inspecteurs des mines, a eu pour objectif de former les inspecteurs du travail au contrôle dans le secteur de la construction (BTP) et à formuler, sur une base participative, une planification stratégique pilote dans ce secteur. De manière spécifique, les participants ont :\nété formés sur le travail de l’inspecteur dans le domaine du BTP, notamment concernant l’identification des risques ;\nété mis en situation professionnelle afin d’observer et pratiquer dans le cadre d’un exercice, deux visites de contrôle de chantiers BTP ;\nconçu une planification stratégique et un plan d’action pour promouvoir la conformité dans le secteur du BTP sur une durée de 6 mois ;\nDans son mot de bienvenue, l’Inspecteur Général du Travail, Madame Marie-Rose Djumba Moseka, a remercié les personnalités et les partenaires présents. Elle a félicité les participants, pour leur présence après avoir été sélectionnés au sein de la Task Force de l’Inspection du Travail. Elle a formulé le vœu de voir les inspecteurs participer aux sessions et travailler assidûment en vue de capitaliser cette formation qui fera d’eux des modèles de la redynamisation et formateurs de l’inspection du travail. Ensuite, elle a rappelé que le système d’inspection du travail connaît beaucoup de défis qui ne lui permettent pas de jouer pleinement son rôle conformément aux dispositions du Code du Travail. Cependant, l’important, après avoir reconnu les faiblesses, est d’en déterminer les causes afin de repartir du bon pied pour rectifier la situation. Parmi les actions correctives planifiées, a-t-elle poursuivi, elle a cité l’investissement dans la formation des inspecteurs du travail dans une approche de formation des formateurs par les pairs.\nPrenant la parole à son tour, M. Roger Nkambu Mavinga, Conseiller Technique Principal et Représentant la directrice du Bureau pays de l’OIT/Kinshasa, a souligné que l’inspection du travail est un instrument essentiel pour la promotion du travail décent. En effet, les inspecteurs sont les autorités publiques de référence pour vérifier la conformité des lieux de travails avec le code du travails dans tous les domaines dont la santé et la sécurité au travail, le dialogue social, la durée du travail ; la protection sociale, la lutte contre le travail illégal, les problématiques liées au VIH, le travail des enfants, la migration de la main d’œuvre, etc. Dans de nombreux pays, les services d’inspection du travail sont des catalyseurs de réformes économiques et sociales. Ils sont des vecteurs et des acteurs de la paix sociale, condition de productivité et de compétitivité.\nIl a indiqué à l’intention des inspecteurs qu’au-delà des aspects techniques de leur métier, il y a l’aspect moral et éthique de leur profession. Il a insisté sur le fait que les inspecteurs sont des promoteurs de valeurs et des agents de changement qui doivent interroger et repenser leurs méthodes et pratiques face aux importants changements et défis qui les attendent. En plus de la formation théorique en salle et des sessions pratiques sur le terrain, l’atelier a permis aux inspecteurs de développer un plan stratégique de l’inspection qui servira de plan d’action pour 2023 avec des indicateurs de performance clairement établis.\nLe financement est fourni par le département du Travail des États-Unis sous le numéro d’accord de coopération IL-35632-75-K. 100% des coûts totaux du projet sont financés par des fonds fédéraux, pour un total de 3 millions de dollars. Ce matériel ne reflète pas nécessairement les points de vue ou les politiques du département du Travail des États-Unis, ni ne mentionne les noms commerciaux, les produits commerciaux ou les organisations impliquent l’approbation du gouvernement des États-Unis.\nLiens pertinents\nLancement Atelier: Articles de presse (écrite et en ligne) Forum des As n°6833 du mardi 10 janvier 2023, page 7 :\nhttps://www.forumdesas.net/2023/01/seize-inspecteurs-du-travail-a-l’ecole-de-planification-strategique-pilote/\nFaire avancer la justice sociale, promouvoir le travail décent\nL'OIT est une agence spécialisée des Nations Unies\nOrganisation internationale du Travail\n4 route des Morillons\nCH-1211 Genève 22\nSuisse\nNous contacter\nSuivez-nous sur\nInstagram", "excerpt_sha256": "872ae5059a678f2e810f307a852c0da338fa4985d04d27a1cd836590f03bb174", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0024-E003", "source_id": "SRC-3efa9a433eb24ee5", "url": "https://actualite.cd/index.php/2024/12/22/rdc-felix-tshisekedi-promulgue-le-projet-de-loi-portant-reddition-des-comptes-pour", "title": "RDC : Félix Tshisekedi promulgue le projet de loi portant reddition des comptes pour l'exercice 2023", "publisher": "Actualite.cd", "raw_sha256": "c8ed6d0cba78e255a077c8d0ac361e09f7a92455fbb995c69e954c1370a6341e", "fact_summary": "During parliamentary review of the 2023 accounts, deputies and senators criticized incoherent spending, emergency procedures, weak provincial credit allocations, and noncompliance with the public-finance law.", "question_ids": ["5B.3", "5B.5", "5B.7", "8B.8"], "polarity": "adverse", "period_fit": "The findings concern execution of the 2023 budget and were raised during its later parliamentary review.", "ruler_attribution": "The criticisms target the government and budget execution; L'annonce a été faite à l'issue d'une série d'ordonnances lues le samedi 21 décembre 2024 sur la chaîne nationale RTNC.\n\"Voté en équilibre, tant en recettes qu’en dépenses, à 14 milliards USD, le budget de l’État pour l’exercice 2023 a été exécuté à hauteur de 13 milliards en recettes, soit un taux de réalisation de 91,22 %, et de 13,86 milliards en dépenses, soit un taux d’exécution de 96,49 %\".\nCe texte a été présenté à l'Assemblée nationale et au Sénat par le ministre des Finances, Doudou Fwamba. Dans ses interventions, le ministre était revenu sur plusieurs points, notamment : la synthèse du projet de loi, la situation consolidée des financements, et les principales articulations du projet. Il avait rappelé que la loi de finances pour l'exercice 2023 avait été exécutée dans un contexte marqué par l'organisation du quatrième cycle électoral en République démocratique du Congo, la poursuite de la guerre d'agression dans l'Est, et la mise en œuvre de divers programmes de développement, tels que le PDL-145T, la gratuité de l’enseignement de base, la couverture santé universelle, et la gratuité de la maternité, entre autres.\nLors du débat général, les sénateurs, tout comme les députés à l'Assemblée nationale, s'étaient montrés très critiques envers le gouvernement sur l'exécution de cette loi de finances. De nombreux élus ont dénoncé des incohérences, des dépenses effectuées en procédure d'urgence, une gestion problématique des provinces avec des faibles taux de crédits alloués, et l'absence de respect des textes en vigueur, notamment la Loi de finances publiques (LOFIP).\nIls ont également pointé, sur la base des observations de la Cour des comptes, des fautes de gestion, telles que des dépassements budgétaires excessifs dans certains portefeuilles, le paiement de dettes non certifiées, et la mention dans le texte de travaux prétendument réalisés alors qu'aucune action concrète n'avait été observée sur le terrain. The state radio and TV broadcaster,\nRadio Télévision Nationale Congolaise (RTNC)\n, the UN’s\n\nRadio Okapi\nand\nTop Congo\nradio have national coverage. The print media are almost non-existent outside the capital, Kinshasa. News websites such as\nActualité.cd\nand\n7sur7.cd\nare in full development.\nPolitical context\nThe Congolese media landscape is marked by the strong presence of politicians who own or launch media outlets intended to promote their influence and rise to power. The national radio and TV broadcaster is a state media outlet that lacks independence. It is very common for local authorities, militias, religious groups, and politicians to exert pressure on journalists and media outlets present in their province. The M23 has ordered some media outlets to modify their editorial policies.\nLegal framework\nThe 1996 press law was revised in April 2023 based on the recommendations of a national media convention, which called for a more modern and more protective legal framework for journalists and the media. The new law tightens the conditions for access to the profession of journalist. It does not explicitly abolish prison sentences for press offences, but it adds a clause of bad faith in the publication of false information or allegations that have disturbed public order. In addition, it guarantees journalists access to information of public interest that is not classified and does not involve state security or national defence.\nEconomic context\nCongolese journalists and media outlets lead a very precarious existence. Employment contracts are rare and the practice of “coupage” – whereby journalists receive payment in exchange for covering an event or reporting some information – is widespread. The funding that the state has to legally provide to media outlets has never been distributed in a transparent manner. Very few media outlets are viable and independent, and most are influenced by those who back them.\nSociocultural context\nJournalists are sometimes targeted on the basis of their ethnic or community affiliation, and they are exposed to reprisals in connection with their work, particularly in the east of the country, where many armed groups are present. The conflict in Nord-Kivu is off-limits for the media, which are caught between the violence of the M23 rebels and the army’s response. Some radio stations and broadcasts were suspended in 2021 for “incitement to tribalism and violence”. Self-censorship is a common practice for many journalists. Covering subjects such as major corruption cases and certain mining contracts are especially difficult.\nSafety\nJournalists are exposed to many dangers in the DRC, including arrests, attacks, threats, forced disappearances, executions, as well as the suspension, looting and ransacking of media offices. Security forces are involved in many abuses and enjoy complete impunity. In Nord-Kivu, journalists have been threatened by a wave of harassment and reprisals since the beginning of 2023 despite a ceasefire. M23 rebels have ordered some media outlets to change their editorial policies. Discouraging the armed forces via the media in wartime is punishable by death. During elections, journalists have been threatened and attacked by politicians and their supporters.\nSupport us\nYour donations enable RSF to keep working. We depend on you in order to be able to monitor respect for press freedom and take action worldwide.\nMake a donation\nBecome a member\nMedia freedom is a fundamental right, but nearly half of the world’s population has no access to freely reported news and information.\nWe need you. September 22, 2023—The Committee to Protect Journalists condemns the charging and ongoing detention of journalist Stanis Bujakera Tshiamala and calls on Congolese authorities to immediately and unconditionally release him.\nOn September 8, police officers\narrested\nBujakera, a reporter for the privately owned Jeune Afrique news website and Reuters, as well as deputy director of publication for the local news website Actualite.cd, and accused him of “spreading false rumors” and “disseminating false information.”\nAuthorities subsequently charged Bujakera with spreading falsehoods, forgery, the use of forged documents, and distributing false documents, according to his lawyer, Hervé Diakiese, and\nnews\nreports\n.\nThe Kinshasa-Gombe court is expected to rule on a request by Bujakera’s lawyer for the journalist’s provisional release on Monday, September 25, according to\nnews\nreports\n.\n“DRC authorities must drop all legal proceedings against journalist Stanis Bujakera, release him, and reform the country’s laws to ensure journalists are never arrested or jailed for their work,” said Angela Quintal, CPJ’s Africa program coordinator, from Durban, South Africa. “The additional charges against Bujakera, who is internationally renowned for his reporting, are only the most recent, glaring example of the harassment and criminalization faced by DRC journalists.”\n\nOn outlet\nsaid\nhe did not write.\nBujakera has been charged under the combined application of the DRC’s digital code, press law, and penal code, which all\ncriminalize the online sharing\nof (AFP/Sébastien Kitsa Musayi)\nAlerts\nCongolese soldiers arrest, beat 3 journalists covering land dispute\nJune 30, 2023 1:50 PM EDT\nShare this:\nShare on Bluesky (Opens in new window)\nBluesky\nShare on Facebook (Opens in new window)\nFacebook\nShare on LinkedIn (Opens in new window)\nLinkedIn\nShare on X (Opens in new window)\nX\nShare on WhatsApp (Opens in new window)\nWhatsApp\nEmail a link to a friend (Opens in new window)\nEmail\nShare this:\n\nShare on Bluesky (Opens in new window)\nBluesky\nShare on Facebook (Opens in new window)\nFacebook\nShare on LinkedIn (Opens in new window)\nLinkedIn\nShare on X (Opens in new window)\nX\nShare on WhatsApp (Opens in new window)\nWhatsApp\nEmail a link to a friend (Opens in new window)\nEmail\nKinshasa, June 30, 2023—Authorities in the Democratic Republic of the Congo must hold accountable the soldiers who arrested and beat journalists Jeef Ngoyi, Marie-Louise Malou Mbela, and Jiresse Nkelani, and cease detaining journalists covering the news, the Committee to Protect Journalists said Friday.\nOn Wednesday, June 28, at least 12 soldiers from the Armed Forces of the DRC (FARDC) arrested, punched, and used belts to hit Ngoyi, a reporter with the U.N.-backed Radio Okapi; Malou, a reporter with state owned Radio Télévision Nationale Congolaise (RTNC); and Nkelani, a camera operator in training with the state owned broadcaster RTNC2.\nThe three journalists had been covering a land dispute in Kinshasa, the capital, according to a\nreport\nas well as Malou and Fiston Wavingana, an RTNC camera operator who witnessed the incident, both of whom spoke to CPJ.\nAuthorities released Malou later that day, and released Ngoyi and Nkelani on Thursday after interventions by the U.N. mission to the DRC, according to a\ntweet\nby a local journalist and a Radio Okapi staffer who spoke to CPJ on the condition of anonymity, citing safety concerns. None of the journalists were charged with a crime.\n“DRC authorities should hold accountable those responsible for arresting and violently abusing journalists Jeef Ngoyi, Marie-Louise Malou Mbela, and Jiresse Nkelani,” said Angela Quintal, CPJ’s Africa program coordinator, in New York. “The repeated arrests and attacks on Congolese journalists by security forces that are supposed to be protecting the public make for an alarming pattern that must be reversed.”\nBefore they were attacked, the journalists had interviewed a man who alleged that\nsoldiers evicted him\nfrom his home on the orders of the DRC Minister of Urban Planning and Habitat.\nhttps://twitter.com/stanysbujakera/status/1674162356635443200?s=46\n\nA\nvideo\nposted on Twitter by a local journalist appears to show Ngoyi, Malou, and Nkelani after their arrest, sitting in the back of a truck with at least two other men, who CPJ was unable to identify. Two soldiers armed with rifles stand over them, and one of the soldiers can be seen beating them with a coiled rope.\nDuring their detention, soldiers held the journalists at the local office of the Military Detection of Anti-Patriotic Activities, an intelligence unit known by its French acronym DEMIAP.\nNgoyi told CPJ that they were not seriously injured by the beatings.\nSylvain Ekenge, a spokesperson for FARDC, claimed the journalist had attacked the soldiers, resulting in their arrest.\nA local DEMIAP official who was reached by phone declined to give their name or comment.
